@@7418654 <h> Sponsors <h> The legacy of US Supreme Court Justice Antonin Scalia <h> US Supreme Court Justice Antonin Scalia died on the night of February 13th , 2016 , at a luxury resort in West Texas , ending his 30-year career as a member of the high court . He was 79 years old . As one of justices in the court 's conservative majority , he has been part of major efforts to stall initiatives by the Obama administration on issues such a climate change and immigration . Overall , the Justice had a well-known career thanks to his traditional viewpoints . <p> As one of the most prominent proponents of ' originalism , ' -- a conservative legal philosophy that believes that the US Constitution should be interpreted the way it would have been understood when it was originally adopted -- Justice Scalia held many unpopular opinions and played a role in a number of controversial rulings by the Supreme Court in recent years . A devout Roman Catholic , Scalia was an outspoken opponent of abortion and gay rights , as well as an advocate @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ possess a handgun ; those opinions were exhibited in his judgments in many high profile cases . In nearly all of his opinions , he mentioned the Constitution , often times amassing criticism , even from fellow judges , for his old-fashioned views . For example , Richard Posner , a judge in the US Seventh Circuit court , had called Scalia 's opinion in the case of District of Columbia v. Heller " faux originalism " and a " historicizing glaze on personal values and policy preferences . " <p> In the 2008 case District of Columbia v. Heller , the Supreme Court was considering a challenge to the gun laws in the area . Scalia wrote the majority opinion , which came to be known as ' The Scalia majority , ' expressing that , under the Second Amendment of the US Constitution , an individual has a right to own a firearm . Despite dissenting opinions pointing out that the Amendment states its purpose specifically in relation to state militias and does not address the right to use firearms in self-defence , the Scalia majority relied on the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ ' militia ' referred to all able-bodied men who were capable of being called to such service . Since this ruling , over 80 different cases have been heard in lower federal courts on the constitutionality of gun control laws and have all been held as lawful . <p> Another area where Scalia enforced his traditional conservative views was in abortion . In the 1992 case of Planned Parenthood v. Casey , the constitutionality of provisions in the Pennsylvania Abortion Control Act were challenged by the Planned Parenthood in the state . It was argued that through Roe v. Wade , which held that abortion was a fundamental right protected by the Due Process Clause of the Fourteenth Amendment , the provisions in the Pennsylvania Abortion Act were unconstitutional because they imposed serious restrictions on a woman 's right to obtain an abortion . As he had done in many previous cases , such as Webster v. Reproductive Health Services , Scalia called upon his colleagues to strike down Roe v. Wade and hold that abortion is not constitutional . Unlike District of Columbia v. Heller , Scalia was not successful @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ other case . <p> While there is an accepted belief that certain characteristics can be given extra consideration when it comes to areas ( such as education ) in order to promote diversity and ensure that minorities are on the same playing field as others , Justice Scalia generally voted to strike down laws that make distinctions by race , gender , or sexual orientation . For example , in the landmark 1996 case of United States v. Virginia in which the Supreme Court struck down the Virginia Military Institute 's long-standing male-only admission policy , Scalia filed a lengthy lone dissent . In the 2003 case of Grutter v. Bolinger , the Supreme Court case that upheld the affirmative action admissions policy of the University of Michigan Law School , Scalia wrote a scathing dissent and mocked the Court majority 's finding that the school was entitled to continue using race as a factor in admissions to promote diversity and increase cross-racial understanding . He argued that the University 's quota system was unconstitutional . Furthermore , in the 1995 case of Romer v. Evans in which the Supreme Court @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ status based upon sexual orientation did not satisfy the Equal Protection Clause of the Fourteenth Amendment to the Constitution , Scalia wrote the minority dissent . He believed that a previously ruling in another case that held that homosexual sodomy was not protected by the right of privacy and thus could be criminally prosecuted by the state should be upheld and that states had a right to pass measures like the ones under consideration in the Romer case . <p> Another area where Scalia 's originalist views came into play was on the highly controversial topic of the death penalty . He believed that the death penalty is constitutional and often dissented in cases where it was held that it was unconstitutional for the death penalty to be applied to those who were under the age of 16 ( as in Thompson v. Oklahoma , 1988 ) and those who have an intellectual disability ( as in Atkins v. Virginia , 2002 ) . Particularly in the Atkins case , Scalia specifically mentioned that it would not have been considered cruel or unusual to execute the mildly intellectually disabled at the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , essentially emphasizing his originalist viewpoint and his opposition to the idea of a living constitution ( in which the interpretation of the Constitution depends more on present-day context and adapts to a changing society ) . <p> Regardless of whether you view his decisions and opinions as conservative and old-fashioned or constitutional and historically accurate , the death of Scalia brings an end to his famous 30-long year career in Supreme Court . His passing is certainly a shock to many .

@@7428416 <p> When Matthew Lutton 's stage production of Joan Lindsay 's cult novel Picnic at Hanging Rock appears at the Malthouse Theatre in Melbourne next week , it will not be merely a debut , but also something of a theatre victory . <p> The title alone has the kind of mass , instant brand-recognition yearned for by any theatre 's marketing department . Lindsay 's 1967 novel about a group of white schoolgirls who disappear into a mountain on Valentine 's Day in 1900 has been of seminal influence on the Australian cultural psyche , especially since Peter Weir 's 1975 film adaptation received world wide acclaim . Forty years after cries of " Miranda ! Miranda ! " echoed among the panpipes of the Weir treatment , Lutton 's Malthouse team were but one of several Australian theatre companies competing for the rights to reproduce the story for the stage . <p> Lutton may be the youngest artistic director of a major performing arts company in Australia , but in addition to his impressive critical achievements , his reputation for diplomacy is legendary ; interviewed , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Instead , he affirms his gratitude that it was Malthouse 's pitch for the rights that won the day . It was Lutton 's good fortune that Lindsay 's literary estate is handled by the same administrators as that of Patrick White 's , who had granted Lutton the rights for a rare production of White 's Night on Bald Mountain at Malthouse in 2014 . They were -- to his relief -- suitably impressed with what they saw . <p> " What we offered was to be faithful to the novel and to Lindsay 's language , " Lutton says of his adaptation , which has been scripted by Tom Wright . " It would be for female voices . And on stage , five women perform all the parts . " <p> Lutton 's stage conceit is that his ensemble of five female actors appear as contemporary schoolgirls , telling the story in alternating characters . " Each actor plays two or three characters , " he explains . " They each narrate parts , they play different roles , and they become possessed by the characters . " @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the only presence in the play . The unusual dramatic conflict at the heart of Picnic at Hanging Rock , in any adaptation , has always been a tension between the white colonial generation attending the picnic , and the Hanging Rock itself , representing the ancient , mysterious Australian continent that white society vainly attempts to control . <p> Unlike his Night on Bald Mountain , which represented an impressive ziggurat-like structural monolith onstage , Lutton 's Hanging Rock is not depicted so literally ; he 's characterised the mysterious landscape that obsesses , possesses and disappears the girls as a metaphoric darkness . <p> " We evoke the rock through the language , and we have a deep blackness in the theatre , which acts as the palpable otherness that keeps impacting on the story , " he says . " There are blackouts that cause girls to disappear and reappear , so there 's this configuring and reconfiguring of the girls and objects as they come closer to the rock . " <p> For Lutton , it 's the explicit juxtaposition of Australia 's post-colonial society with the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of Lindsay 's story , as well as the recent competing attempts for its stage realisation . He says that " the constant unpacking of the colonial narrative " offered by Picnic at Hanging Rock has been a consistent interest for local audiences , to whom its metaphoric references -- intrusion into the landscape , and displacement within it -- are readily understood . <p> The character of the remote Miranda exudes a particular fascination for both the characters within the story and audiences beyond it , says Lutton , because she reconciles herself to her surroundings . " The story of Miranda is of someone who intuitively understands land , and that 's what makes her beautiful . " <p> It 's the specific contemporary challenge of Australia -- to reconcile itself to its land -- that Lutton suggests inspired the recent spate of offers to adapt Picnic 's text to production . In the era of climate change , and fear of a destabilised environment , the story takes on a new relevance . At the time the story is set , says Lutton , white Australian society was @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Lindsay was writing , " it was no longer awe-inspiring -- it was a thing that we were trying to get under control . Now , we 're trying to live in symbiosis with it " . <p> Does Lutton think we ever will ? <p> Ever the diplomat , he demurs . " Picnic at Hanging Rock is a story we 've turned into into a national myth , " says Lutton . " We know the story is n't true but we want to believe it 's true . There 's an enduring need in Australia to build myths on incomplete stories ... I think that 's part of the Australian psyche . " 
@@7428527 <p> The current crash in oil prices is sowing the seeds of a powerful rebound and a potential supply crunch by the end of the decade , but the prize may go to the US shale industry rather Opec , the world 's energy watchdog has predicted . <p> America 's shale oil producers and Canada 's oil sands will come roaring back from late 2017 onwards once the current brutal purge is over , a cycle it described as the " rise , fall and rise again " of the fracking industry . <p> " Anybody who believes the US revolution has stalled should think again . We have been very surprised at how resilient it is , " said Neil Atkinson , head of oil markets at the International Energy Agency . <p> The IEA forecasts in its " medium-term " outlook for the next five years that US production will fall by 600,000 barrels per day ( b/d ) this year and 200,000 next year as the so-called " fracklog " of drilled wells is finally cleared and the global market works off a surplus @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to life within six months - far more quickly than conventional mega-projects and offshore wells - once crude rebounds to $60 . Shale output is expected to reach new highs of 5m b/d by 2021 . <p> This will boost total US production of oil and liquids by 1.3m b/d to the once unthinkable level 14.4m b/d , widening the US lead over Saudi Arabia and Russia . <p> Fatih Birol , the IEA 's executive director , said this alone will not be enough to avert the risk of a strategic oil crisis later in the decade , given the exhaustion of existing wells and the dangerously low levels of spare capacity in the world . <p> " Even if there were zero growth in demand , we would have to produce 3m b/d just to stand still , " he said , speaking at the IHS CERAWeek summit of energy leaders in Texas . <p> Mr Birol said investment in oil exploration and production across the world has been cut to the bone , falling 24pc last year and an estimated 17pc this year . This is a drop @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ minimum levels needed to keep up with future demand . <p> " It 's not good news for oil security . Over the past 30 years we have never seen oil investment dropping two years in a row , " he said . <p> " It is easy for consumers to be lulled into complacency by ample stocks and low prices today , but they should heed the writing on the wall : the historic investment cuts raise the odds of unpleasant oil security surprises in the not too distant future , " he said . <p> The warnings were echoed by Opec 's secretary-general , Abdalla El-Badri , who said the current slump will lead to serious trouble when the cycle turns . " It sows the seed for a very high price in the future , " he said at the CERAWeek forum . <p> Mr El-Badri said he had lived through six oil cycles over his career but the surge of shale oil supply from the US has made this one of the most vicious . " It is a supply bubble . This cycle is very nasty @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ that the cartel has been caught badly off guard by crash , blaming the wild moves on speculative forces with control over 5m " paper barrels " on the derivatives markets . " The fundamentals have not changed that much , " he said . <p> But Mr El-Badri sent mixed signals about the real problem in the crude markets , letting slip that Opec and the US shale industry may not be able to " live together " and that frackers will take advantage of output cuts intended to stabilize the market . " If there is any increase in price , shale will come back immediately , " he said . <p> Contrary to widespread assumptions , the IEA report said Saudi Arabia and the Opec club will lose market share , treading water as North America and Brazil 's " pre-salt " basin in the Atlantic account for most of the growth in global output by the early 2020s . Algeria , Venezuela , Nigeria and Indonesia are all going into decline . <p> Iran 's grand plan to reach 5m b/d and regain its place as the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . It will struggle to add much once it has recaptured its pre-sanctions level of 3.6m b/d . Iran 's major fields are 70 years old and need sophisticated technology , yet foreign investors are wary of taking the plunge . <p> Outside Opec , there will be a steady erosion of output in China , Mexico , Colombia , Egypt , Oman and the North Sea , all chipping away at global supply and leaving the world vulnerable as demand rises by an average of 1.2m b/d each year - hitting 100m b/d by 2020 . <p> China 's demand will ratchet upwards by an accumulated 2.5m b/d even as its own output slips , a scissor effect likely to tighten the global market relentlessly from 2017 onwards . <p> The IEA report implicitly calls into question Opec 's strategy of flooding the market in order to cripple of the US shale industry . Asked if the policy had failed , Mr Birol deflected the question diplomatically . <p> " I would n't could call it failure of this group or that group , but there is a new fact @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ It is the success of oil industry , " he said . <p> While the Opec strategy is finally forcing frackers to shut down , it has taken far longer than expected and may prove fleeting since private equity groups armed with a $60bn war chest are waiting to buy up the assets of failed shale companies . <p> The strategy has been prohibitively costly for Opec itself . Annual revenues have dropped from a peak of $1.2 trillion to around $400bn at today 's prices , and a large part of this is a result of Opec 's own actions . <p> The IEA said US frackers have been able to cut costs by 25pc-30pc and even more in the Permian Basin of West Texas . " A year ago it was widely believed that this would happen by the end of 2015 but that view has proved to be very wide of the mark . In 2014 and again in 2015 supply exceeded demand by massive margins , " it said . <p> Much of the confusion is over the US " rig-count " , which has dropped from @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ nearly as quickly as the rig-count alone would suggest , " it said . <p> Russia is perhaps the biggest casualty , given that it is trying to fund a superpower military status and cover half its budget comes from oil and gas revenues . @@7445537 <h> Zika and Ebola : A taste of things to come ? <p> Ebola . Zika . Both diseases that were unknown to many until recently . But there have been huge outbreaks of both - and each time scientists and global health experts were caught off guard . <p> In this week 's Scrubbing Up , Dr Seth Berkley , CEO of the Gavi , the Vaccine Alliance suggests Ebola and Zika may be followed by other public health emergencies fuelled by other lesser-known diseases . <p> First it was Ebola and now Zika ; two official World Health Organization ( WHO ) Public Health Emergencies of International Concern within as many years . <p> Both diseases have been known about for decades , and yet in both cases no vaccines or drugs were available when we most needed them . So what 's going on ? <p> Is this just a terrible coincidence , being caught off-guard like this twice in such quick succession , or is it part of a worrying trend and a taste of things to come ? <p> At first glance that @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ common . One is difficult to catch but a ferocious killer , while the other spreads with ease but is relatively harmless to the vast majority of people infected . <p> Yet , in both cases there is something novel , either in the way the virus has spread or in how it affected people which has made the outbreaks more of a threat . <p> In global health security terms that is a real concern , because such sudden changes of modus operandi can not only make public health threats even more difficult to predict or anticipate than normal , but also make all the difference between a localised outbreak and global pandemic . <p> Even more worrying is the fact that with changing trends in human and animal migration , increasing urbanisation , the density of mega cities , the rise in antimicrobial resistance and climate change , such threats could become increasingly more common . <p> In the case of Ebola , what changed was its ability to spread . <p> Historically Ebola 's aggression has been its own worst enemy ; the virus often immobilising and killing @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , limiting its spread mainly to contact with the deceased . <p> Image copyrightSimon Davis/Department for Inter <p> Because of this , for decades it remained a relatively low impact disease , confined to small outbreaks in remote and relatively unpopulated rural regions . <p> What changed in West Africa was that for the first time it was able to reach more densely populated urban areas , increasing its ability to spread almost exponentially . <p> With Zika it was different . It had been believed to be a relatively benign disease , producing only mild flu-like symptoms - if any at all . <p> Because of this there was little concern about the spread of this mosquito-borne disease as it crossed continents . <p> But now with a Zika outbreak suspected as the most likely cause of a sudden spike in cases of microcephaly in Brazil - which can cause babies to be born with abnormally small heads - we have another global health emergency on our hands , particularly if reports of sexual transmission prove valid and its spread is not limited to mosquitoes . <p> If Zika is @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ why . In the seven decades since Zika was first discovered , such horrific complications have never before been observed . <p> A form of nerve damage , called Guillain-Barr ? syndrome , has been seen a small number of people , and in the case of pregnancies there were 17 cases of malformations of the central nervous system in foetuses following an outbreak in French Polynesia in 2014 . <p> However , even then Zika was not implicated until recently , and only after the alarm was sounded in Brazil . <p> So , why now ? It could simply be something we only see as a result of scale - 1.5m cases of Zika in Brazil , compared to just 30,000 in the worst previous outbreak . <p> Or it could just be that surveillance in Brazil was good enough to detect it , picking up both increases of Zika and microcephaly immediately , compared to West Africa where poor health systems meant it took three months before Ebola was first confirmed . <p> Or it could be that the virus has mutated to a more virulent strain . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ years before we establish a conclusive link with microcephaly , and possibly even longer before we understand the epidemiological factors leading to its sudden emergence now . <p> Image copyrightScience Photo Library <p> What is clear is that in addition to mosquito control in affected countries what may also be needed is another new vaccine . <p> But unlike Ebola , we do n't have several candidate vaccines lined up , waiting in the wings which we can rapidly steer through clinical trials . <p> Thanks to Ebola , industry has now been faster to react and commit to develop a vaccine or adapt existing ones , but it will still likely be years before one is ready . <p> However , why does it take a global health emergency for us to even realise no vaccine exists in the first place ? <p> Part of the problem is that for some serious diseases there is simply no profit in prevention , which means that if we want to avoid epidemics we can not expect industry to provide the solution . <p> Instead governments , public funders and private donors need @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ so now , rather than waiting until the next epidemic . <p> The good news is that we now already have an idea of where to focus our attention . In December the WHO brought together scientists and clinicians who came up with an " initial list " which reads like a most-wanted of the worst eight diseases , including Ebola and other haemorrhagic fevers like Marburg and Lassa fever . <p> They also flagged a sub-set of three other serious diseases that also needed urgent attention , which included Zika . <p> And therein lies the point . None of the diseases on the list were particularly surprising . <p> They are known threats , it 's just that they are not big enough threats to have warranted the world to rally round and put in place the incentives to develop vaccines , at least not yet . <p> What Zika and Ebola have both taught us is that we ca n't assume pathogens will continue to behave the same way . <p> We need to stop waiting until we see evidence of a disease becoming a global threat before @ @ @ @ @ @

@@7446009 <p> It is a three-hour , thigh-torturing climb to reach Tasmania 's high central plateau . Ancient myrtle rainforests flank the slopes . In years gone by , springs and streams gushed from the soaked highlands above , feeding the ferns and tall , old trees . The track passes Norm 's spring , from which local legend holds it is good luck to drink . But in these parts the luck has run dry . <p> For the world heritage-listed ecosystem above , these normally sodden forests are a fortification against the fires that perennially torch the lowlands . But their fluorescent mosses turned a circumspect pastel green in the heat . By the time December and January broke summer heat records , they were just waiting for a spark . <p> On 13 January a huge , dry electrical storm set more than 70 fires rampaging across the island . Within days the flames tore through the dried-out defences and into the world heritage area above . For more than a month , fire has rolled back and forth across the fragile plains . <p> At @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ plants once marked the northern edge of Tasmania 's vast world heritage area . These fragile plant communities build on the skeletal wood of their ancestors . As the centuries pass they construct huge , alien-green mounds that bulge from the peat . Today they look like a tray of burnt sponge cakes . <p> The beginning of its end is a theft from us all . Two-thirds of the plant species exist nowhere else on earth <p> A long glacial valley stretches out below , devoid of colour , filled only with twisted black branches and burned stones ; a monument to entropy . The rocks still radiate heat even though a fortuitous cloudburst put out the flames weeks before . At every step the normally spongy soil bursts into puffs of dust . The torched bark of thousand-year-old pencil pines shines iridescent black . <p> Only in tiny pockets has some life survived . Due to some inherent extra wetness , a protecting rock or a random swirl of the wind , here and there a few square metres of peat still shout forth little fantasias of sphagnum moss @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Like funeral photos of a young life cut short , these still-glorious toeholds only accentuate the bitter , irredeemable tragedy of the surrounding acres of ash . <p> When vandals of Islamic Stateblew up the temples of Palmyra , the sickened world responded with appropriate and universal rage . The director general of Unesco , the UN body that oversees world heritage sites , called it a " war crime " . <p> As the burning of Tasmania enters its sixth week , Unesco remains silent . But if the dire warnings of forest scientists are correct , this summer heralds a new era of decline for this great Gondwana ecosystem . Unlike eucalyptus forests , these plants have not evolved to cope with regular bushfires . Once burned , they die . In a region that has rarely experienced fire , the blackened trunks of millennial trees will burn again and again . Some fire-resistant species will remain but the change will be absolute . <p> The beginning of its end is a theft from us all . Two-thirds of the plant species on the plateau exist nowhere else on @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of the world 's remaining pencil pines -- among the longest living of all trees -- have been lost in these blazes . <p> The British explorer Gertrude Bell once wondered of Palmyra 's temples whether " the wide world presents a more singular landscape " . For the pilgrims who visit Tasmania 's wild sanctuary , there is only one answer . They come , from both near and far , to worship a different articulation of the divine . Or , as one local bushwalker puts it : " This is where we go to have fun . " <p> Away to the west , fires still burn . Tasmania 's fire service says they are contained . Thankfully , the weather failed to produce the dire northerly buster that would have sent the fires deep into the wilderness . Damage has been limited to 22,000 hectares of the vast 1.5m-hectare park . The Tasmanian government has been at pains to point out that the great majority remains intact . Rather than the final cataclysm , the ruined valleys at the northern edge are a premonition of a warmer @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ burnt cushion plants near Lake Mackenzie in late January . Photograph : Rob Blakers <p> Unesco says it is " not in a position to speculate about the extent to which global warming is responsible for this particular fire " . Earlier this month the Tasmanian premier , Will Hodgman , attacked " activists " for " almost gleefully capitalising " on the fires , which he said were " naturally caused " . <p> But this ignores the unnatural rarity of these particular fires and the circumstances that preceded them . If they had been lit by arsonists , says David Bowman , a forest ecologist from the University of Tasmania , " that would be bad , but you would understand that that was preventable " . <p> Instead the rising background of climate change combined with a huge El Ni ? o to create conditions in which peat bogs were dry enough to burn for the first time in perhaps a thousand years . Tasmania 's rainfall has been decreasing since the 1970s , accompanied by a rise in annual average temperature of half a degree . Last @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ o in effect , the occurrence of Australia 's three hottest-recorded springs in the past three years was " almost certainly " caused by greenhouse gases in the atmosphere . Climate scientists have also predicted that lightning strikes will happen 12% more frequently with every degree of warming . <p> " It 's a historically significant event , " Bowman says . <p> Deep in the valley , a tiny grove of pines is still green . From afar the trees ( which look to be about 500 years old -- young by pencil pine standards ) appear to have been protected from the fire by a rocky slope . But closer inspection reveals that the peat burned right up to their bases . Licking flames singed the bark at the bottom of their trunks . Then , inexplicably , the fire turned away . Perhaps this was the moment the rain came to douse the flames . The chubby needles of the pines remain soft and lively . But if these trees are going to ride luck like this their end can not be far away . <p> The parents @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the citizens of Palmyra still walked their desert streets . The razing of these old treasures are two points on the wide spectrum of human failure . Along with the fractured temple of Baalshamin , Isis have broken the statues of Hatra in Iraq . In Afghanistan , the Taliban blasted the great Buddhas of Bamiyan . All of these places carried the aegis of world heritage . This week news comes that the violence of climate change has turned towards another Unesco wonder , the Great Barrier Reef . <p> At the edge of the central plateau , a half-torched wooden signpost bears Unesco 's world heritage symbol , signifying the interdependence of nature and human ingenuity . Nothing within eyeshot is now worthy of such lofty recognition . Amid the blackened clumps of the cushion plants , the icon is a travesty .

@@7446275 <h> The True Value of the Environment <p> The True Value of the Environment <p> Many people , especially younger generations , argue that placing a price on carbon will reduce societies ' reliance on fossil fuel and incentivise the private sector to invest in renewable energy . However , following a recent setback in Obama 's policy in a $10 per oil barrel tax , implementation of a tax on carbon seems increasingly unlikely . <p> Climate change has been a hot topic in the recent decade , with increasing evidence stacking against climate sceptics , attitudes among the population have begun to shift towards taking more actions to mitigate climate change . According to recent data published by the Met Office , the global average temperature in 2015 was the highest on record since pre-industrial age , thrashing the previous record set in 2014 by a substantial margin . The driver of this temperature increase is attributed to anthropogenic emissions of greenhouse gases , such as carbon dioxide , methane and various multi-halogenated carbon compounds . With the temperature fast approaching the much publicised 1 ? @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the threshold value of 2 ? C is apparent , yet , there seems to be a distinct lack of intergovernmental cooperation to take collective responsibility in solving the crisis . As evident in the Paris Climate Conference , developing countries , such as China and India , will continue to prioritise economic growth over taking action on climate change . So , how will this impact our lives in the future ? <p> One of the effects associated with climate change is the rise in the global temperature . This allows the atmosphere to hold more water vapour that can result in the increased risk associated with severe weather events and the consequential economic damages . Examples of severe tropical storms , such as Hurricane Katrina in 2005 and Hurricane Sandy in 2012 , have costs the US economy around $108 billion and $71.4 billion , respectively . Other effects , such as rising sea level , desertification , habitat change , and species extinction , all have their associated costs . So who is responsible for this ? Surprisingly , this is a very hard question to answer . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ term Environmental Debt . Amy Larkin , Vice-Chair of the World Economic Forum 's Global Agenda Council on Climate Change , was the first to relate the cost of environmental disaster to the health of businesses and economies . The example she commonly refers to is the flooding in Thailand in 2011 . As a result of logging activities in the late 20th century , the ability of soils to absorb the floodwater reduced . Consequently , the flooding experienced was intensified and has resulted in the closure of manufacturing plants in the region . This impacted the supply chain of car manufacturers , such as Toyota and Honda , and caused auto-part shortages for late stage manufacturing . Subsequently , the production was suspended in the US , Singapore , and the Philippines , which caused the loss of output for Toyota and Honda ( 3.4% in annual output ) , as well as the loss of 260,000 jobs in regions , affected . In effect , Toyota and Honda , several governments , and the local population all paid for the environmental debt of the logging companies , who @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the case above , it seems obvious that there is an identifiable cause to the intensified flooding experienced . However , as we look at an increasingly interconnected world brought about by globalisation , corporations can transfer the responsibility of environmental protection and liability associated from one country to another . For example , by setting up manufacturing plants in China , where there are more relaxed emission controls , corporations from developed countries can effectively push the environmental burden onto China . As the demand for policy changes intensifies there , increases in legislations will undoubtedly make it less attractive for corporations to locate in the region . The most cost effective solution for corporations is often to find an alternative location , and , therefore , continue to push the responsibility to another country and avoid tackling the problem . This is evident for the chemical sector in the EU , where increasingly difficult regulations resulted in increased operating cost and cost associated with compliance , which reduced the competitiveness of the chemical industry within the EU . This resulted in the decreased global market share of EU chemical @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ companies opt for the more cost-effective solution to increasing operations in US and Asia , rather than invest in finding solutions through research and development . <p> In the world of finance , the economic health of a country or corporation is often judged upon its profits or losses , with assets and liabilities being an integral part of that analysis . However , as seen with the examples above , one 's assets often translate to liabilities for others . A starting point in addressing this problem is to establish effective environmental cost reporting in corporations ' financial statements through governmental policies . This will encourage environmental , social governance in corporations , which aims to eliminate the out-dated thinking of economical benefit over environmental protection , and begin to recognise the importance of sustainability and the potential economic benefits it can bring in the future .

They are also directly affected by the consequences of climate change -- such as rising sea levels , ravaging storms and increased temperatures .

Finally , at the crowd reached critical mass , various speakers came forward to make their case for unilateralism . <p> Kate Hudson , the General Secretary of CND , introduced the speakers by saying the gathering was the single-largest demonstration against nuclear weapons in a generation . The leftwing activist and actor , Vanessa Redgrave , said it was the happiest day of her life . Other speakers included Leanne Wood ; Nicola Sturgeon ; various religious leaders , including the Bishop of Chelmsford , Rt Revd Stephen Cottrell ; union representatives ; and Labour leader , Jeremy Corbyn . <p> For me , three main themes emerge from what I heard . One , many speakers said that nuclear weapons belong to the past , and serve no purpose in the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ now , including terrorism and climate change , are not addressed by a weapons system from the Cold War . Caroline Lucas , the Greens only sitting MP , called Trident " a reckless vanity project that makes us less safe , not more safe . " " It makes us less safe , " she said , " because of accidents and miscalculations . It makes us less safe because it sends a message to the rest of the world that security depends on nuclear weapons . " <p> The second main theme was that the vast sums of money that Trident costs would be better spent on public services such as the NHS , nationalising steel mills and building wind turbines , and building new homes . The sum floated by the CND as the total cost of Trident is ? 183 billion over its lifespan . <p> Lastly , many speakers spoke of the changing mood of the British public . Despite the mainstream media 's aggressively hostile treatment of the peace movement , they said , the British public are now largely opposed to the UK maintaining @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Kate Hudson told the crowd . " We are now a majority . " At one point I Tweeted out that Vanessa Redgrave had just called for a referendum on Trident and the NHS . To my total surprise , dozens and dozens of people liked and re-tweeted the comment , including the account of longtime socialist and Labour activist , Harry Leslie Smith . <p> Walking home , questions came to mind . For one , on the point of public support , recent polling actually shows that more than half of Britons support Trident 's renewal . If a referendum were held , it would divert political energy away from other crucial parliamentary activity , and cost tens of millions of pounds to officiate . We 're already facing a referendum this year , and it would be a drain on party resources , and MPs ' time , to force them to campaign on a Trident referendum . It should be noted that in the general election the two main parties campaigned on pro-Trident platforms . <p> Next , while I 'm still not entirely convinced that nuclear @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ it 's unlikely any money saved on nuclear weapons could be invested into public services . NATO still requires the UK to put 2% of GDP into defense spending . If we did n't invest in Trident , we would still have to invest in the military , unless we withdrew from NATO . 
@@7460972 <p> It was n't all foregone conclusions at the Oscars 2016 . As anticipated , the race issue dominated much of the night and Leo nailed his Best Actor gong . But the Best Picture winner was an upset , as was the Best Supporting Actor prize - which went to one of Britain 's greatest stage actors instead of a Hollywood old timer . These are the eight things that everyone will be talking about this morning . <h> 1 . Spotlight wins Best Picture against the odds <p> Just when it looked certain that The Revenant was going to run away with Best Picture , the Academy sprung a surprise . Tom McCarthy 's forensic look at a newspaper expos ? of child abuse by the Catholic Church is as unshowy as it is gripping . It also chimed with a theme forcefully conveyed by the unlikely pairing of US Vice President Joe Biden and Lady Gaga earlier in the evening : that the horror of sexual abuse must stop . <h> 2 . Inevitable wins for DiCaprio and Larson <p> If there was one copper-bottomed @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ : Leonardo DiCaprio and Brie Larson would triumph in the Best Actor and Actress categories . Mark Rylance wins Best Supporting Actor <p> All the talk prior to the Oscars regarding the Best Supporting Actor category was about Sylvester Stallone , whose steely work in Creed made him the clear favourite . And yet it was celebrated British thesp Rylance who defied the odds , winning for Bridge of Spies and becoming only the second person to pick up an acting award in a Spielberg movie ( the other actor was Daniel Day Lewis for Lincoln ) . ' Let 's change the culture . " <p> Lady Gaga then performed her song , as victims of sexual abuse joined her on stage . @@7461176 <p> A 100-metre high wind turbine is set to start generating power at the headquarters of the Royal Society for the Protection of Birds , powering almost 500 homes . <p> Construction of the turbine has been completed at the RSPB 's Lodge headquarters near Sandy , Bedfordshire , by energy company Ecotricity . <p> The turbine , which will save an estimated 800 tons of carbon emissions a year as it generates the equivalent of half the electricity the RSPB uses across its 127 sites , is part of moves by Europe 's largest nature conservation charity to make its operations greener . <p> Pursuing wind power is not without controversy for the RSPB , as critics of the technology warn that turbines pose a threat to birds , particularly rare species that are already suffering from low numbers or migratory species , as well as to bats . <p> But an article in the journal Nature a few years ago pointed out that turbines killed far fewer birds than other human causes such as buildings , cars , power lines , pesticides and pet cats . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ biggest threat " to birds and other wildlife and says it has been involved in hundreds of wind-farm applications to make sure local bird nesting activity , migratory patterns and flight paths are taken into account . <p> The charity said it had worked in partnership with Ecotricity on three years of detailed ecological and environmental research to confirm the location at its headquarters was suitable for a turbine . <p> RSPB director of conservation Martin Harper said : " Climate change is the single biggest threat to our planet . This is about our birds and wildlife as well as our way of life . <p> " Around the world , and even in the UK , we can already see how these changes are affecting wildlife , the places where they live as well as causing damage to our homes and disruptions to the economy . <p> " It is down to everyone to play their part . In the UK we have the potential to generate a significant portion , if not all , of our electricity from sustainable sources . " <p> He added : " Using @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ greenhouse-gas emissions . But turbines must be located where they are sympathetic to our natural environment . " <p> Ecotricity has financed and installed the wind turbine , which will produce power at a discounted cost to the charity . Any excess electricity that is generated is then sold by the energy company in a scheme which also powers operations by Sainsbury 's , Ford and B&amp;Q . <p> Ecotricity founder Dale Vince said the partnership allowed the company to work with customers to make green energy where they needed it and to share the benefits . <p> " Green energy puts power in the hands of the people ; the technology allows us to democratise and decentralise energy in Britain . Green energy is also a strong economic and an environmental choice . It is about jobs and the new industrial revolution -- about building a truly sustainable economy in Britain . "

@@7461532 <p> Reassuringly , at the anti-nuclear demo in London on Saturday , Jeremy Corbyn was wearing the same beard he had a generation ago . His speech had changed . He described nuclear warheads as " weapons of mass destruction " whereas we used to talk about mass annihilation . Broadly , though , he still passed as that creature the anti-nuclear campaigner has always been : the throwback ; the person whom voters have rejected , modernity has superseded , real life has rendered obsolete . You could say the same about Caroline Lucas ; Greens have been anti-nuclear since before we discovered climate change and all we worried about was acid rain . <p> And yet , there were disturbing new elements to the demonstration , breaches in the image of anti-nukery as nostalgic and irrelevant . First , the presence of a stack of people who were n't born in the 1980s , making arguments that did n't exist at that time . <p> Second , the sight of the Scottish first minister , Nicola Sturgeon , a walking , talking contradiction of the maxim @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to be amended to read : " abolishing Trident -- polling booth kryptonite to the British people in perpetuity , except the Scottish , who appear to take to it pretty quickly , all things considered " ) . <p> The context that built the pro-nuclear argument no longer exists <p> Finally , there 's the fact that the demonstration happened at all ; we gave up protesting nuclear proliferation because the norm was too well-defended . You might not feel safer with a seat at the nuclear table , but your countrymen do ; so just deal with it . Concentrate on the fights you might win . Have a climate march instead . Yes , it was defeatist , but it was an untroubled defeatism -- there was never any shortage of things to march against . Taking Trident off the table was n't the end of the world ( until it was the end of the world ) . <p> Some of the new energy comes from the injection of fresh blood : people politicised by student fees , junior doctors ' contracts , or any one of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ think more deeply about public spending and what its priorities do to the lives we lead . There is also the fact that the marginalisation of this issue relied on a deliberate misrepresentation of the implications of opposing Trident . <p> This was , and continues to be , portrayed as a value of the hard-left ; as such , it was part of the extreme socialism that made Labour unelectable for so long , and from which Tony Blair gallantly and generously rescued the party . In reality , of course , it is a legitimate moral and philosophical position to say that the threat to life contained within this weaponry is too great to be justified by the interests of any nation or any ideology . Trident is like the death penalty : to be in favour of these things is quite hard-right authoritarian ; to be opposed is really very soft left , if not pretty centrist . The love affair in some quarters of the Labour party with nuclear arms is more about their crisis of confidence than proof that nuclear capacity is essential to the social @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of this movement , which are far more profound than the Labour party 's new leadership , although that has doubtless brought it into focus . The context that built the pro-nuclear argument no longer exists . Nowadays we are not nation states pitted against one another , our aggression held at bay by a rational understanding of mutually assured destruction . Our enemies -- at least if we are to believe the rhetoric of our own heads of state -- are death cults , to whom the possibility of a nuclear attack would be not so much a deterrent as an incentive . <p> We have a different understanding of the planet now , even if we can only intermittently demonstrate that in our behaviour . The cosy glow of security you get from having a nuclear capacity only works if , in the final analysis , you can conceive of an attack on another territory as their problem , not yours . As soon as you start to consider the global consequences , any hostile nuclear event -- anywhere -- is a tragedy for all of us . It @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . This puts us in a hall of mirrors , watching the solidity of deterrence disintegrate . <p> And while no defence programme has ever been formulated in a time of limitless public spending , the current economic context exposes the expense of Trident to ridicule . Can a nation whose health service , its pride and joy , has become " unsustainable " ( Jeremy Hunt 's implication , not mine ) really afford to spend so much deterring an enemy it ca n't even identify , whose susceptibility to such pressure is impossible to know or to guess ? Does a nation really need international status as a potential bringer of Armageddon more than it needs to educate its citizens , freely , at a tertiary level ? <p> It 's possible that there are people answering these questions with a throaty " yes " , but you do n't need to be very far to the left to answer " no " . The Trident lobbyists may get new arguments -- " what about the jobs ? " one hears a bit these days , as though this @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ useful labour market lever . But their old lines have stopped working . <p> The world changed and they did n't . They are the dinosaurs ; they are the warhorses ; they are the dreamers , living with yesterday 's truths .

@@7469310 <p> The Oscar is an inflexible little statuette , yet it has always bent over backwards to accommodate any number of causes and campaigns , enabling actors and directors to pretend that they are setting political agendas . <p> But has the self-importance of the Academy Awards ever scaled such towering heights as it did in Los Angeles on Sunday evening ? <p> From African-American host Chris Rock 's non-stop jokes about racial prejudice , to Leonardo DiCaprio 's dire warnings about global warming , to Spotlight producer Michael Sugar sending a message to Pope Francis about abusive priests , to Sam Smith 's idiotic claim to be the first openly gay man ever anointed with an Oscar , it was as if all the sanctimonious posturing down the years had finally erupted in one vast explosion of self-regarding pomposity . <p> And then there was Lady Gaga , performing her ballad Till It Happens To You , from a documentary about rape on university campuses . <p> As she warbled away , dozens of grim-faced survivors of sexual abuse surrounded her white piano , and the entire @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ song , but also , let 's be frank , themselves , for being such elegantly-dressed standard-bearers for justice and decency . As far as any piece of theatre quite that contrived can be moving , it was . But the rush of luvvie adrenaline began almost immediately . <p> ' These survivors . Wow . Floored by the courage . And beauty . And realness , ' tweeted actress Kerry Washington . Realness ? Who was she kidding ? I love the Oscars , but practically everything about them is illusory , up to and including the idea that films as diverse as The Revenant , Spotlight , Brooklyn and Mad Max : Fury Road can honestly and reliably be compared . <p> But then , ever since the Academy Awards began in 1929 , there have been plenty of instances of Hollywood folk taking themselves far too seriously . Perhaps the most egregious example came in 1973 , when Marlon Brando declined his Best Actor trophy for The Godfather , and instead sent his friend Sacheen Littlefeather , a civil rights activist , to deliver a speech about the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Share <p> Three years ago , Jared Leto used his acceptance speech as Best Supporting Actor for Dallas Buyers ' Club to castigate Russian policies towards the Ukraine , as if all of Crimea were breathlessly following events in the Dolby Theatre . Of course , there 's no doubt that tens of millions of people do watch the Academy Awards . Nor is there any doubt that actors and film-makers , for better or worse , are role models . And it 's commendable that they use their influence to draw attention to evil and injustice , and not simply to their over-priced frocks and over-the-top rocks . <p> But the Dolby Theatre stage is not a soapbox and there is a limit to how much lecturing we should be expected to take . The 88th Academy Awards greatly exceeded that limit , showcasing almost more causes than films . <p> The irony was that these had been billed as the politically incorrect Oscars , following the apparent snubbing of black actors and directors -- the so-called #OscarsSoWhite controversy . <p> Actor Leonardo DiCaprio ( right ) made dire warnings @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ while singer Sam Smith ( left ) made an ' idiotic claim ' to be the first openly gay man ever anointed with an Oscar <p> From the start of Sunday 's ceremony , it was apparent that practically everyone present was desperate to make amends , and to show that they were n't the ones to blame for overlooking black talent ( even though rather a lot of them must have been ) . <p> Hence the riotous laughter when Chris Rock quipped that if hosts had to be nominated he would never have got the gig . <p> African-Americans did n't usually care about white talent being favoured , he said , because they were ' too busy being raped and lynched to care about who won Best Cinematographer ' . The point was well made but he promptly overdid it with a variation of the same gag . ' You know , when your grandmother 's swinging from a tree , it 's really hard to care about Best Documentary Foreign Short . ' OK , Chris . Enough already . <p> If it was predictable that Rock @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ even deader cert that DiCaprio , in the odds-on event of him stepping up to receive a first Best Actor Oscar for his performance in The Revenant , would use his big moment to draw attention to his pet cause : the environment . <p> ' Climate change is real , it 's happening right now , it 's the most urgent threat affecting our entire species , ' he thundered , the camera briefly flitting to his friend Kate Winslet in the audience , filling up with actressy empathy and pride . <p> Significantly , unlike everyone else who prattled on for too long , DiCaprio was not interrupted by the orchestra . <p> But Leo at least comported himself with dignity . The same , alas , can not be said for Britain 's Sam Smith , who won Best Original Song for his Bond theme . <p> Referring to the race row , Chris Rock quipped that if hosts had to be nominated he would not have got the gig <p> Hollywood 's homophobia was just as bad as its racism , he shrieked , implying that as @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ was a brave pioneer , bursting through some sort of barrier of prejudice . @@7469329 <h> Scotland <h> 70% of Scots ' want renewable energy made government priority ' <p> The majority of Scots want to see the next Scottish Government make renewable energy a priority , according to a poll . <p> Nearly three-quarters of those quizzed , 70% , said they want to see more energy produced from renewables such as wind , solar , wave and tidal power . <p> And two-thirds agreed that the next administration should " continue to take forward policies that tackle greenhouse gas emissions and climate change " . <p> In comparison , around 19% said the next government should prioritise the use of fracking for shale gas , and a third , 33% , said they support new nuclear power plants being treated as a priority . <p> The YouGov poll , commissioned by Scottish Renewables , has been published on the first day of the body 's annual conference in Edinburgh . <p> It comes around two months before voters head to the polls for the Holyrood election on May 5 . <p> Niall Stuart , chief executive of Scottish Renewables , said @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ continue to be strongly behind the growth of renewable energy , with support for the sector way ahead of any other . <p> " Just months after the Paris climate change agreement , the poll also shows clear support for Scotland 's next government to prioritise policies to reduce greenhouse gas emissions . " <p> When asked about their own energy needs , respondents were given the option of picking from a list of renewable energy devices they would consider installing in or around their own homes . <p> Mr Stuart added : " While only 4% of the sample has small scale renewables at home , there is clear appetite amongst householders to generate their own renewable energy . " <p> Friends of the Earth Scotland head of campaigns Mary Church said : " What this poll tells us is that people want strong action on climate change , including renewable energy , and they do n't want unsafe , unnecessary industries like shale gas and coal-bed methane . <p> " Given the urgency of climate science , the growing evidence of the risks associated with fracking , and mounting @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ get to work on delivering a swift and fair transformation to a fossil-free energy system within a generation . " <p> Lang Banks , director of WWF Scotland , said : " When given the choice it 's clear that the public would rather see more renewables than fossil fuels or nuclear power . The message to politicians ahead of May 's elections is clear - continue to prioritise renewables and commit to ensuring Scotland becomes the EU 's first fully renewable electricity nation by 2030 . <p> " Independent research has shown that it is possible for Scotland to have a secure , efficient electricity system , based on almost entirely renewable electricity generation , by 2030 . Not only is it possible , these new figures show it is by far the most popular public vision for the future . " <p> The poll , in which 1,013 respondents took part in Scotland , was carried out from February 17-22.
